We further show that detoxification effectiveness degrades when multiple unsafe behaviours are edited jointly, and that both monolingual and cross-lingual detoxification remain effective only under specific model-method combinations. Overall, our results indicate that KE-based detoxification is robust only for certain models, limited numbers of detoxification objectives, and a subset of languages.</p></details> |  |

## Sparse Autoencoder
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Xetrieval: Explaining Dense Retrieval through Feature Elicitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29507v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dense retrieval enables effective semantic matching, yet the semantic factors underlying its relevance scores remain opaque. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) support feature-level explanations by decomposing dense embeddings into sparse semantic features. However, the document semantics underlying a retriever's relevance scores may not be sufficiently explicit in the embeddings to be recovered as interpretable SAE features. We introduce Xetrieval, a framework for explaining dense retrieval through feature elicitation. Feature Elicitors make retrieval-relevant aspects of document meaning more explicit through auxiliary views, facilitating their identification as interpretable SAE features. A shared sparse dictionary learns from these varied representations to express query--document relations through describable features. We assess their influence on relevance scores through interventions on the original document embeddings. Experiments show that Xetrieval yields more interpretable features than an SAE baseline and identifies feature sets with a stronger influence on the retriever's scores. Task-level feature modulation further demonstrates more effective retrieval steering. These findings highlight the value of feature elicitation for relevance attribution and retrieval steering.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/Hihiczx/Xetrieval ; Project page: https://hihiczx.github.io/Xetrieval</p></details> |
| **[Interpreting vision transformers via residual replacement model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17401v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How do vision transformers (ViTs) represent and process the world? This paper addresses this long-standing question through the first systematic analysis of 6.6K features across all layers, extracted via sparse autoencoders, and by introducing the residual replacement model, which replaces ViT computations with interpretable features in the residual stream. Our analysis reveals not only a feature evolution from low-level patterns to high-level semantics, but also how ViTs encode curves and spatial positions through specialized feature types. The residual replacement model scalably produces a faithful yet parsimonious circuit for human-scale interpretability by significantly simplifying the original computations. As a result, this framework enables intuitive understanding of ViT mechanisms. Finally, we demonstrate the utility of our framework in debiasing spurious correlations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Disentangled Sparse Representations for Concept-Separated Diffusion Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12122v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unlearning specific concepts in text-to-image diffusion models has become increasingly important for preventing undesirable content generation. Among prior approaches, sparse autoencoder (SAE)-based methods have attracted attention due to their ability to suppress target concepts through lightweight manipulation of latent features, without modifying model parameters. However, SAEs trained primarily for sparse reconstruction do not explicitly enforce concept-wise separation, allowing semantically related concepts to share latent features. To address this, we propose SAEParate, which explicitly separates semantically overlapping concepts in the SAE latent space through concept-aware contrastive learning, supported by cross-attention-guided spatial aggregation that emphasizes concept-relevant regions. In addition, we enhance the encoder with a nonlinear transformation to increase its expressive capacity under this separation objective, enabling more concept-discriminative latent representations. Experiments on UnlearnCanvas demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, with particularly strong gains in joint style-object unlearning, a challenging setting where existing methods suffer from severe interference between target and non-target concepts.</p></details> | <details><summary>53 pa...</summary><p>53 pages, 25 figures. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking SAE Evaluation: An Atomic Interpretable Unit Framework Reveals Hidden Polysemanticity and Redundancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30474v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) were originally motivated by discovering monosemantic features, a property that characterizes individual features but does not specify what the learned feature set should look like as a whole. As SAEs have increasingly been adopted for attribution, circuit discovery, and intervention, researchers have begun evaluating metrics that describe global properties of the feature set, such as sparsity, dead feature rates, reconstruction fidelity, and disentanglement, a diverse set that extends far beyond monosemanticity. However, this implicit shift beyond monosemanticity has not been formalized as a clear, well-defined objective that comes before these evaluation metrics. Without such an objective, existing metrics remain ad hoc, with their necessity unjustified. In this work, we formalize this ultimate objective by introducing a new paradigm for SAE evaluation: requiring that SAE features, and more generally dictionary-based decompositions of model representations, form an atomic interpretable unit basis, a coordinate system for semantically meaningful explanation. Under this paradigm, we organize existing and new measurements such as DAME (Dominance-Aware Monosemanticity Evaluation) as proxy metrics and introduce a comprehensive benchmarking framework. Evaluating hundreds of SAEs across multiple architectures and model families, we uncover failure modes in which some SAEs appear monosemantic under existing evaluation metrics while remaining functionally polysemantic: the most dominant features, those contributing most to model computation, are systematically less interpretable and more redundant. These failure modes warrant attention from SAE researchers as targets for improved training and architectures, and motivate dominance-aware evaluation as a standard check for practitioners before SAE features are deployed for attribution, circuit tracing, or intervention.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track). 92 pages. Code: https://github.com/ritengzhang77-max/SAE-AIU</p></details> |
| **[Semantic Optimal Transport for Sparse Autoencoder Feature Matching and Circuit Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28567v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have become a central tool for interpreting language models. However, two key SAE analyses that remain difficult to scale are (1) matching semantically similar features across multi-layers and (2) compressing large feature circuits into interpretable supernodes. We introduce a distributional framework for this problem, in which each feature is represented not by a single decoder vector like in the literature, but by an activation-weighted distribution over the hidden states that express it. By projecting these distributions into a shared reference space and comparing them with Wasserstein distance, our method provides a unified semantic metric for cross-layer feature comparison. We prove that our representation is invariant to activation rescaling, stable under perturbations, and recovers true matches under finite-sample margin conditions. Empirically, our method outperforms decoder-vector and LLM-based baselines and captures subtle functional distinctions between related features. Notably, our method compresses large feature circuits into interpretable supernodes automatically.</p></details> | Neurips 2026 |
| **[Building Auditable AI Text Detectors with Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30006v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-generated text detectors are increasingly deployed in consequential settings---peer review, education, admissions, journalism, and content moderation---where their outputs directly affect individuals. Yet the strongest detectors are fine-tuned neural classifiers whose decisions are opaque, while transparent alternatives (stylometric and linguistic-feature classifiers) underperform them by wide margins. We accomplish this by training sparse autoencoders (SAEs) directly on a detector's residual stream, assigning auto-interpretation labels to each SAE feature, and retaining only features whose generated descriptions pass a global fidelity threshold. Native-only threshold calibration explains much, but not all of the gap: on five tasks with matched probe scores, it recovers $69.8$\% of the raw native-to-probe difference, while direct routing adds $0.094$ mean macro-F1 beyond calibrated native scoring. Joint gold-label, probe-KL, and pairwise LoRA supervision improves dedicated FHM prediction, but a gold-only adapter performs better on the shared seven-task mean. A case study of Gemma-3-12B on the Facebook Hateful Memes dataset finds a distributed rank-32 image-prompt interaction, reaching $0.756$ versus $0.685$ native macro-F1. Robustness controls show that the signal is not explained solely by accompanying OCR and depends on paired visual evidence, and that it extends beyond English. In many of the errors we study, the evidence is represented but does not reach the answer; therefore, routing is a common bottleneck in harmful meme classification.</p></details> | 42 pages, 9 figures |
| **[How Optimality Structures Sparse Dictionaries: Theory for Interpreting SAE Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02385v2)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) have found success parsing neural network representations into interpretable concepts, providing a basis for understanding and control. However, what exactly SAEs extract and, hence, the scientific conclusions we can draw from them are not obvious. In short, if your SAE behaves strangely, does that reflect interesting neural network behaviour or an SAE-imposed distortion? Towards answering this, we use dictionary learning identifiability results to derive constraints that optimal dictionary learning features must satisfy. For example, an optimal feature will never turn on only while another is active. We use these conditions to explain various SAE oddities - hierarchical splitting & absorption, which features can be left in the residuals, dense antipodal features, and infinite feature splitting - simply as properties imposed by the dictionary learning objective. Finally, these constraints are diagnostic: real SAEs pass when measured on the dataset on which they were trained, but increasingly fail as the test dataset becomes more `distant'. In sum, we hope to provide theoretical tools to explain puzzling SAE patterns, allowing more principled inferences about internal model behaviour.</p></details> | 31 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Do Music Generative Models Understand Musical Qualities? Automatic Music Evaluation with Model-Intrinsic Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37710v1)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current music generative models can produce high-quality music, but does this ability imply that they ``understand'' the musical qualities of their outputs, and is that understanding aligned with human evaluation? Previous attempts to use the likelihood of a generative model to evaluate music, an approach commonly used in text, have proven unsuccessful, leading researchers to rely on standalone supervised music evaluation models. In this paper, we answer this question affirmatively: we show that a model's intrinsic signals---derived from its hidden representations and predictions---are strongly correlated with human ratings. In particular, we study MusicGen and consider three types of features: (1) prediction loss, (2) prediction entropy, and (3) concepts extracted from the model using a sparse autoencoder (SAE). Using these features, we train a lightweight prediction model to estimate subjective ratings. We evaluate these features both individually and in combination. We hypothesize that these signals parallel the listening process: the temporal and frequency-domain structure of loss and entropy reflects listeners' expectation and surprise, while gradient directions in SAE latent space predict perceived quality. Experiments on five human-evaluation benchmarks spanning continuous ratings and pairwise preferences confirm this hypothesis, with SAE latents carrying most of the predictive signal.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the 27th International Society for Music Information Retrieval Conference (ISMIR 2026)</p></details> |
